Yeah… I do. Not always in a big obvious way, but lately I’ve been kind of arranging things so I don’t have to be near them. And at little festivals or school events, if there’s a table with balloons tied to it, I’ll sort of drift away to another area.\n\nI mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just checking on the kids or getting supplies, which sometimes I am, but honestly I’m also trying not to be close to the balloons. If I really have to stay, I keep as much distance as I can and just try to get through it without looking at them.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation where you see balloons and start feeling that chest tightness, do you also feel like it's hard to escape or get out of that situation, which makes the anxiety even worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s the “can’t escape” part exactly… I mean, at school I can usually move somewhere else, or I tell myself I’m just going to grab something from the supply closet. Although then I start thinking about whether I’m being obvious, because the kids notice everything—one time they all got very focused on a glitter jar I dropped, and then the whole morning was just chaos.\n\nBut with balloons, I guess I don’t really think in words like, “I’m trapped.” It’s more like I don’t want to be near it, and I’m very aware of where it is. If I can step away, I do. If I can’t, I just sort of try to keep doing what I’m doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t know if I’d say it’s like I’m losing control exactly… but it is really sudden. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.

It’s silly because I teach little kids, so there are decorations all the time, and I’m usually thinking about snack time or who needs their jacket zipped, but then I spot a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops before I can focus on what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you see a balloon, does your heart start racing or do you feel like you need to get out of the situation right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the first thing I notice is my chest tightens, like immediately, even if the balloon is way down the hallway. I kind of freeze for a second, which is embarrassing because I’m supposed to be watching the kids or remembering who needs what, and then suddenly I’m just stuck staring at this balloon instead of thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or events where you think there might be balloons?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big obvious way, but lately I’ve been kind of arranging things so I don’t have to be near them. Like if there’s a birthday circle at school, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part if there are balloons around. And at little festivals or school events, if there’s a table with balloons tied to it, I’ll sort of drift away to another area.\n\nI mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just checking on the kids or getting supplies, which sometimes I am, but honestly I’m also trying not to be close to the balloons. If I really have to stay, I keep as much distance as I can and just try to get through it without looking at them.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation where you see balloons and start feeling that chest tightness, do you also feel like it's hard to escape or get out of that situation, which makes the anxiety even worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s the “can’t escape” part exactly… I mean, at school I can usually move somewhere else, or I tell myself I’m just going to grab something from the supply closet. Although then I start thinking about whether I’m being obvious, because the kids notice everything—one time they all got very focused on a glitter jar I dropped, and then the whole morning was just chaos.\n\nBut with balloons, I guess I don’t really think in words like, “I’m trapped.” It’s more like I don’t want to be near it, and I’m very aware of where it is. If I can step away, I do. If I can’t, I just sort of try to keep doing what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried in situations that aren’t related to balloons, like during regular school activities or at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in the same way, I guess. I mean, school can be stressful just because there are a million little things happening at once—someone needs help with their shoes, someone spilled paint, someone is crying because their block tower fell over. So I do get frazzled, but that feels kind of normal for the job.\n\nAt home, I’m usually just tired. I’ll worry about whether I remembered to send a note to a parent or wash the snack containers, but… I don’t think it’s the same kind of anxious feeling. It’s hard to separate sometimes, though, because preschool days are just noisy and unpredictable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense wave of fear or discomfort that peaked within minutes when you were near balloons, where you felt like you were losing control or something terrible was going to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like I’m losing control exactly… but it is really sudden. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. What I notice most is that the second I see balloons—like even if they’re just across the school hallway—my chest tightens and I freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts lined back up.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing, which is ridiculous because it might just be, you know, taking the kids to wash hands or checking who has their coat on. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s silly because I teach little kids, so there are decorations all the time, and I’m usually thinking about snack time or who needs their jacket zipped, but then I spot a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops before I can focus on what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you see a balloon, does your heart start racing or do you feel like you need to get out of the situation right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the first thing I notice is my chest tightens, like immediately, even if the balloon is way down the hallway. I kind of freeze for a second, which is embarrassing because I’m supposed to be watching the kids or remembering who needs what, and then suddenly I’m just stuck staring at this balloon instead of thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or events where you think there might be balloons?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big obvious way, but lately I’ve been kind of arranging things so I don’t have to be near them. Like if there’s a birthday circle at school, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part if there are balloons around. And at little festivals or school events, if there’s a table with balloons tied to it, I’ll sort of drift away to another area.\n\nI mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just checking on the kids or getting supplies, which sometimes I am, but honestly I’m also trying not to be close to the balloons. If I really have to stay, I keep as much distance as I can and just try to get through it without looking at them.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation where you see balloons and start feeling that chest tightness, do you also feel like it's hard to escape or get out of that situation, which makes the anxiety even worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s the “can’t escape” part exactly… I mean, at school I can usually move somewhere else, or I tell myself I’m just going to grab something from the supply closet. Although then I start thinking about whether I’m being obvious, because the kids notice everything—one time they all got very focused on a glitter jar I dropped, and then the whole morning was just chaos.\n\nBut with balloons, I guess I don’t really think in words like, “I’m trapped.” It’s more like I don’t want to be near it, and I’m very aware of where it is. If I can step away, I do. If I can’t, I just sort of try to keep doing what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried in situations that aren’t related to balloons, like during regular school activities or at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in the same way, I guess. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel an overwhelming sense that something terrible is about to happen or that you might lose control, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think, like, “something terrible is going to happen” in words. The second I spot balloons at school—even if they’re way down the hallway—my chest tightens and I just freeze for a moment.\n\nThen I’m sort of standing there trying to remember what I was doing, like lining the kids up or checking backpacks, and it takes me a second to think clearly again. Although then I start thinking about whether I’m being obvious, because the kids notice everything—one time they all got very focused on a glitter jar I dropped, and then the whole morning was just chaos.\n\nBut with balloons, I guess I don’t really think in words like, “I’m trapped.” It’s more like I don’t want to be near it, and I’m very aware of where it is. If I can step away, I do. If I can’t, I just sort of try to keep doing what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried in situations that aren’t related to balloons, like during regular school activities or at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in the same way, I guess. I mean, school can be stressful just because there are a million little things happening at once—someone needs help with their shoes, someone spilled paint, someone is crying because their block tower fell over. So I do get frazzled, but that feels kind of normal for the job.\n\nAt home, I’m usually just tired. I’ll worry about whether I remembered to send a note to a parent or wash the snack containers, but… I don’t think it’s the same kind of anxious feeling. It’s hard to separate sometimes, though, because preschool days are just noisy and unpredictable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense wave of fear or discomfort that peaked within minutes when you were near balloons, where you felt like you were losing control or something terrible was going to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like I’m losing control exactly… but it is really sudden. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. What I notice most is that the second I see balloons—like even if they’re just across the school hallway—my chest tightens and I freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts lined back up.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing, which is ridiculous because it might just be, you know, taking the kids to wash hands or checking who has their coat on. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel an overwhelming sense that something terrible is about to happen or that you might lose control, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think, like, “something terrible is going to happen” in words. It’s more that my body reacts before I’ve even caught up. The second I spot balloons at school—even if they’re way down the hallway—my chest tightens and I just freeze for a moment.\n\nThen I’m sort of standing there trying to remember what I was doing, like lining the kids up or checking backpacks, and it takes me a second to think clearly again. Which is hard, because in preschool there’s always something happening, someone needing help with a zipper or asking where the purple marker went… but yeah, the balloon thing just interrupts everything right away.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel like everything around you isn’t real, or like something really bad is about to happen to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like things look unreal or like I’m outside of myself. I’m not sure I’d describe it as “something bad is about to happen to me,” either.\n\nIt’s more just—well, balloons are everywhere at school sometimes, especially birthdays, and then there are all the little decorations people bring in, and I’m trying not to make it obvious because the kids get so excited about them. But no, I haven’t really noticed that unreal feeling you’re describing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that many things all happening at once. Like, I know I tense up and kind of stop for a second, but sweating or trembling or choking— I don’t really know.

Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. What I notice most is that the second I see balloons—like even if they’re just across the school hallway—my chest tightens and I freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts lined back up.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing, which is ridiculous because it might just be, you know, taking the kids to wash hands or checking who has their coat on. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel an overwhelming sense that something terrible is about to happen or that you might lose control, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think, like, “something terrible is going to happen” in words. It’s more that my body reacts before I’ve even caught up. The second I spot balloons at school—even if they’re way down the hallway—my chest tightens and I just freeze for a moment.\n\nThen I’m sort of standing there trying to remember what I was doing, like lining the kids up or checking backpacks, and it takes me a second to think clearly again. Which is hard, because in preschool there’s always something happening, someone needing help with a zipper or asking where the purple marker went… but yeah, the balloon thing just interrupts everything right away.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel like everything around you isn’t real, or like something really bad is about to happen to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like things look unreal or like I’m outside of myself. I’m not sure I’d describe it as “something bad is about to happen to me,” either.\n\nIt’s more just—well, balloons are everywhere at school sometimes, especially birthdays, and then there are all the little decorations people bring in, and I’m trying not to make it obvious because the kids get so excited about them. But no, I haven’t really noticed that unreal feeling you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness and freezing up, you also experienced at least three other symptoms all at once—like sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling dizzy, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that many things all happening at once. Like, I know I tense up and kind of stop for a second, but sweating or trembling or choking— I don’t really know.\n\nIt’s funny, because at school there are so many distractions that sometimes I’m more focused on keeping the kids from grabbing things or popping decorations, and then I’m thinking about whether I remembered snack count or if someone’s parent sent in cupcakes again. So I might not be paying attention to every body symptom, if that makes sense.\n\nBut no, I can’t really say there’s been a clear episode where I had three or more of those things all at once.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m sorry, I know you asked that already in a slightly different way. I don’t really have a clear answer beyond… I haven’t noticed three or more things like that happening all at once.

It’s silly because I teach little kids, so there are decorations all the time, and I’m usually thinking about snack time or who needs their jacket zipped, but then I spot a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops before I can focus on what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you see a balloon, does your heart start racing or do you feel like you need to get out of the situation right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the first thing I notice is my chest tightens, like immediately, even if the balloon is way down the hallway. It’s hard to separate sometimes, though, because preschool days are just noisy and unpredictable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense wave of fear or discomfort that peaked within minutes when you were near balloons, where you felt like you were losing control or something terrible was going to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like I’m losing control exactly… but it is really sudden. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. What I notice most is that the second I see balloons—like even if they’re just across the school hallway—my chest tightens and I freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts lined back up.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing, which is ridiculous because it might just be, you know, taking the kids to wash hands or checking who has their coat on. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel an overwhelming sense that something terrible is about to happen or that you might lose control, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think, like, “something terrible is going to happen” in words. It’s more that my body reacts before I’ve even caught up. The second I spot balloons at school—even if they’re way down the hallway—my chest tightens and I just freeze for a moment.\n\nThen I’m sort of standing there trying to remember what I was doing, like lining the kids up or checking backpacks, and it takes me a second to think clearly again. Which is hard, because in preschool there’s always something happening, someone needing help with a zipper or asking where the purple marker went… but yeah, the balloon thing just interrupts everything right away.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel like everything around you isn’t real, or like something really bad is about to happen to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like things look unreal or like I’m outside of myself. I’m not sure I’d describe it as “something bad is about to happen to me,” either.\n\nIt’s more just—well, balloons are everywhere at school sometimes, especially birthdays, and then there are all the little decorations people bring in, and I’m trying not to make it obvious because the kids get so excited about them. But no, I haven’t really noticed that unreal feeling you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness and freezing up, you also experienced at least three other symptoms all at once—like sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling dizzy, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that many things all happening at once. Like, I know I tense up and kind of stop for a second, but sweating or trembling or choking— I don’t really know.\n\nIt’s funny, because at school there are so many distractions that sometimes I’m more focused on keeping the kids from grabbing things or popping decorations, and then I’m thinking about whether I remembered snack count or if someone’s parent sent in cupcakes again. So I might not be paying attention to every body symptom, if that makes sense.\n\nBut no, I can’t really say there’s been a clear episode where I had three or more of those things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness when seeing balloons, you also experienced at least three other symptoms at the same time, like sweating, shaking, shortness of breath, dizziness, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you asked that already in a slightly different way. I don’t really have a clear answer beyond… I haven’t noticed three or more things like that happening all at once.\n\nWhen I see balloons, I definitely get stuck for a second and I’m aware of my chest, but then my brain usually jumps to, like, “Okay, where are the kids, is anyone about to touch it, do I need to move them along,” and then someone’s asking for help with their shoes or there’s glitter all over the table. So I’m not sitting there taking inventory of every symptom.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a specific episode where I was also sweating and shaking and dizzy or choking or all of that together. I just don’t know that I’ve had it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

If I really have to stay, I keep as much distance as I can and just try to get through it without looking at them.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation where you see balloons and start feeling that chest tightness, do you also feel like it's hard to escape or get out of that situation, which makes the anxiety even worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s the “can’t escape” part exactly… I mean, at school I can usually move somewhere else, or I tell myself I’m just going to grab something from the supply closet. Although then I start thinking about whether I’m being obvious, because the kids notice everything—one time they all got very focused on a glitter jar I dropped, and then the whole morning was just chaos.\n\nBut with balloons, I guess I don’t really think in words like, “I’m trapped.” It’s more like I don’t want to be near it, and I’m very aware of where it is. If I can step away, I do. If I can’t, I just sort of try to keep doing what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried in situations that aren’t related to balloons, like during regular school activities or at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in the same way, I guess. I mean, school can be stressful just because there are a million little things happening at once—someone needs help with their shoes, someone spilled paint, someone is crying because their block tower fell over. So I do get frazzled, but that feels kind of normal for the job.\n\nAt home, I’m usually just tired. I’ll worry about whether I remembered to send a note to a parent or wash the snack containers, but… I don’t think it’s the same kind of anxious feeling. It’s hard to separate sometimes, though, because preschool days are just noisy and unpredictable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense wave of fear or discomfort that peaked within minutes when you were near balloons, where you felt like you were losing control or something terrible was going to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like I’m losing control exactly… but it is really sudden. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. What I notice most is that the second I see balloons—like even if they’re just across the school hallway—my chest tightens and I freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts lined back up.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing, which is ridiculous because it might just be, you know, taking the kids to wash hands or checking who has their coat on. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel an overwhelming sense that something terrible is about to happen or that you might lose control, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think, like, “something terrible is going to happen” in words. It’s more that my body reacts before I’ve even caught up. The second I spot balloons at school—even if they’re way down the hallway—my chest tightens and I just freeze for a moment.\n\nThen I’m sort of standing there trying to remember what I was doing, like lining the kids up or checking backpacks, and it takes me a second to think clearly again. Which is hard, because in preschool there’s always something happening, someone needing help with a zipper or asking where the purple marker went… but yeah, the balloon thing just interrupts everything right away.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel like everything around you isn’t real, or like something really bad is about to happen to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like things look unreal or like I’m outside of myself. I’m not sure I’d describe it as “something bad is about to happen to me,” either.\n\nIt’s more just—well, balloons are everywhere at school sometimes, especially birthdays, and then there are all the little decorations people bring in, and I’m trying not to make it obvious because the kids get so excited about them. But no, I haven’t really noticed that unreal feeling you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness and freezing up, you also experienced at least three other symptoms all at once—like sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling dizzy, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that many things all happening at once. Like, I know I tense up and kind of stop for a second, but sweating or trembling or choking— I don’t really know.\n\nIt’s funny, because at school there are so many distractions that sometimes I’m more focused on keeping the kids from grabbing things or popping decorations, and then I’m thinking about whether I remembered snack count or if someone’s parent sent in cupcakes again. So I might not be paying attention to every body symptom, if that makes sense.\n\nBut no, I can’t really say there’s been a clear episode where I had three or more of those things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness when seeing balloons, you also experienced at least three other symptoms at the same time, like sweating, shaking, shortness of breath, dizziness, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you asked that already in a slightly different way. I don’t really have a clear answer beyond… I haven’t noticed three or more things like that happening all at once.\n\nWhen I see balloons, I definitely get stuck for a second and I’m aware of my chest, but then my brain usually jumps to, like, “Okay, where are the kids, is anyone about to touch it, do I need to move them along,” and then someone’s asking for help with their shoes or there’s glitter all over the table. So I’m not sitting there taking inventory of every symptom.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a specific episode where I was also sweating and shaking and dizzy or choking or all of that together. I just don’t know that I’ve had it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or having physical symptoms like chest tightness in situations that aren't related to balloons—like during regular school activities, at home, or in other environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve really noticed in a clear way. Regular classroom stuff can be stressful, obviously—like when everyone needs something at the same time, or someone spills paint right after we cleaned up, or there’s a fire drill and half the class is crying—but I wouldn’t say I get that same chest feeling just from normal school things.\n\nAt home, I don’t think I notice it either. I mean, I can get tired or overwhelmed like anyone, especially after a long day with little kids, but… no, I can’t really point to physical symptoms happening in other situations the way they do when balloons are around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… I guess I do think ahead about it sometimes. Like if I know there’s going to be a birthday in class, lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circle part, which feels a little silly because it’s just part of the day, but… if there might be balloons, I’d rather not be the one right next to them.

And with school festivals or those little tables they set up—sometimes they’ll have balloons tied to signs or chairs, and I’ll sort of drift to the other side or find something else to do. It’s funny, because there’s always so much going on at those things, like parents asking where the bathrooms are or kids spilling juice, so I can make it look like I’m just busy.

If I absolutely have to stay there, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at the balloons too much.

I’m not sure I’ve had it in that really sudden, all-at-once way you’re describing. Like, I know with balloons I can get really uncomfortable and kind of tense, but when you list all those things—sweating, trembling, heart racing, dizziness, feeling like I’m dying—I don’t know that I can honestly say, yes, four of those happened together.

If I can step away, I do. If I can’t, I just sort of try to keep doing what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried in situations that aren’t related to balloons, like during regular school activities or at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in the same way, I guess. I mean, school can be stressful just because there are a million little things happening at once—someone needs help with their shoes, someone spilled paint, someone is crying because their block tower fell over. So I do get frazzled, but that feels kind of normal for the job.\n\nAt home, I’m usually just tired. I’ll worry about whether I remembered to send a note to a parent or wash the snack containers, but… I don’t think it’s the same kind of anxious feeling. It’s hard to separate sometimes, though, because preschool days are just noisy and unpredictable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense wave of fear or discomfort that peaked within minutes when you were near balloons, where you felt like you were losing control or something terrible was going to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like I’m losing control exactly… but it is really sudden. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. What I notice most is that the second I see balloons—like even if they’re just across the school hallway—my chest tightens and I freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts lined back up.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing, which is ridiculous because it might just be, you know, taking the kids to wash hands or checking who has their coat on. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel an overwhelming sense that something terrible is about to happen or that you might lose control, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think, like, “something terrible is going to happen” in words. It’s more that my body reacts before I’ve even caught up. The second I spot balloons at school—even if they’re way down the hallway—my chest tightens and I just freeze for a moment.\n\nThen I’m sort of standing there trying to remember what I was doing, like lining the kids up or checking backpacks, and it takes me a second to think clearly again. Which is hard, because in preschool there’s always something happening, someone needing help with a zipper or asking where the purple marker went… but yeah, the balloon thing just interrupts everything right away.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel like everything around you isn’t real, or like something really bad is about to happen to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like things look unreal or like I’m outside of myself. I’m not sure I’d describe it as “something bad is about to happen to me,” either.\n\nIt’s more just—well, balloons are everywhere at school sometimes, especially birthdays, and then there are all the little decorations people bring in, and I’m trying not to make it obvious because the kids get so excited about them. But no, I haven’t really noticed that unreal feeling you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness and freezing up, you also experienced at least three other symptoms all at once—like sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling dizzy, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that many things all happening at once. Like, I know I tense up and kind of stop for a second, but sweating or trembling or choking— I don’t really know.\n\nIt’s funny, because at school there are so many distractions that sometimes I’m more focused on keeping the kids from grabbing things or popping decorations, and then I’m thinking about whether I remembered snack count or if someone’s parent sent in cupcakes again. So I might not be paying attention to every body symptom, if that makes sense.\n\nBut no, I can’t really say there’s been a clear episode where I had three or more of those things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness when seeing balloons, you also experienced at least three other symptoms at the same time, like sweating, shaking, shortness of breath, dizziness, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you asked that already in a slightly different way. I don’t really have a clear answer beyond… I haven’t noticed three or more things like that happening all at once.\n\nWhen I see balloons, I definitely get stuck for a second and I’m aware of my chest, but then my brain usually jumps to, like, “Okay, where are the kids, is anyone about to touch it, do I need to move them along,” and then someone’s asking for help with their shoes or there’s glitter all over the table. So I’m not sitting there taking inventory of every symptom.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a specific episode where I was also sweating and shaking and dizzy or choking or all of that together. I just don’t know that I’ve had it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or having physical symptoms like chest tightness in situations that aren't related to balloons—like during regular school activities, at home, or in other environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve really noticed in a clear way. Regular classroom stuff can be stressful, obviously—like when everyone needs something at the same time, or someone spills paint right after we cleaned up, or there’s a fire drill and half the class is crying—but I wouldn’t say I get that same chest feeling just from normal school things.\n\nAt home, I don’t think I notice it either. I mean, I can get tired or overwhelmed like anyone, especially after a long day with little kids, but… no, I can’t really point to physical symptoms happening in other situations the way they do when balloons are around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about seeing balloons even when you’re not currently around them—like thinking ahead to school events or planning your day around the possibility of balloons being there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do think ahead about it sometimes. Like if I know there’s going to be a birthday in class, lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circle part, which feels a little silly because it’s just part of the day, but… if there might be balloons, I’d rather not be the one right next to them.\n\nAnd with school festivals or those little tables they set up—sometimes they’ll have balloons tied to signs or chairs, and I’ll sort of drift to the other side or find something else to do. It’s funny, because there’s always so much going on at those things, like parents asking where the bathrooms are or kids spilling juice, so I can make it look like I’m just busy.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay there, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at the balloons too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment when you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort along with four or more of the following things happening at the same time: sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling your heart race, chest pain, nausea, dizziness, chills or hot flashes, numbness, feeling detached from reality, or feeling like you were losing control or dying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve had it in that really sudden, all-at-once way you’re describing. Like, I know with balloons I can get really uncomfortable and kind of tense, but when you list all those things—sweating, trembling, heart racing, dizziness, feeling like I’m dying—I don’t know that I can honestly say, yes, four of those happened together.\n\nSometimes it’s hard because in the classroom everything is so busy. If there’s a birthday thing, I’m also thinking about who needs to sit down, who’s about to grab the ribbon, whether the cupcakes have nuts, you know… so I’m not exactly tracking my body closely.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a moment where it was suddenly that intense with four or more of those symptoms all at the same time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Like if I know there’s going to be a birthday in class, lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circle part, which feels a little silly because it’s just part of the day, but… if there might be balloons, I’d rather not be the one right next to them.\n\nAnd with school festivals or those little tables they set up—sometimes they’ll have balloons tied to signs or chairs, and I’ll sort of drift to the other side or find something else to do. It’s funny, because there’s always so much going on at those things, like parents asking where the bathrooms are or kids spilling juice, so I can make it look like I’m just busy.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay there, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at the balloons too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment when you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort along with four or more of the following things happening at the same time: sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling your heart race, chest pain, nausea, dizziness, chills or hot flashes, numbness, feeling detached from reality, or feeling like you were losing control or dying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve had it in that really sudden, all-at-once way you’re describing. Like, I know with balloons I can get really uncomfortable and kind of tense, but when you list all those things—sweating, trembling, heart racing, dizziness, feeling like I’m dying—I don’t know that I can honestly say, yes, four of those happened together.\n\nSometimes it’s hard because in the classroom everything is so busy. If there’s a birthday thing, I’m also thinking about who needs to sit down, who’s about to grab the ribbon, whether the cupcakes have nuts, you know… so I’m not exactly tracking my body closely.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a moment where it was suddenly that intense with four or more of those symptoms all at the same time.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel that your reaction to balloons is out of proportion to any actual danger they pose, and has this reaction been happening consistently for at least six months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / preschool_or_kindergarten_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, but when I see one stretched tight or bobbing near the children, it feels like it could burst right in my face and I get scared in a way that seems bigger than the situation.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, logically I know it’s out of proportion. A balloon in the classroom is usually just decoration, or it’s tied to a chair for someone’s birthday, and the kids are excited about it. It’s not like it’s actually dangerous in the way my body seems to react to it.

But when I see one that’s stretched really tight, or it’s bobbing around near the children—especially if they’re waving it or pulling on the string—I just get this feeling like it could pop right in my face any second. And then I feel scared in a way that feels… bigger than the situation, I guess.

As far as the six months part, I’d have to think a little—I’m bad with timelines, honestly. The school year kind of blurs together, especially with all the holidays and classroom themes and decorations. But it has been happening consistently enough that I’ve noticed it and started adjusting what I do around balloons.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.290, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because at school, when I see balloons—like even if they’re just down the hallway—my chest tightens right away and I kind of freeze for a second. It’s silly because I teach little kids, so there are decorations all the time, and I’m usually thinking about snack time or who needs their jacket zipped, but then I spot a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops before I can focus on what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you see a balloon, does your heart start racing or do you feel like you need to get out of the situation right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the first thing I notice is my chest tightens, like immediately, even if the balloon is way down the hallway. I kind of freeze for a second, which is embarrassing because I’m supposed to be watching the kids or remembering who needs what, and then suddenly I’m just stuck staring at this balloon instead of thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or events where you think there might be balloons?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big obvious way, but lately I’ve been kind of arranging things so I don’t have to be near them. Like if there’s a birthday circle at school, I’ll ask one of my aides to handle that part if there are balloons around. And at little festivals or school events, if there’s a table with balloons tied to it, I’ll sort of drift away to another area.\n\nI mean, I’ll tell myself I’m just checking on the kids or getting supplies, which sometimes I am, but honestly I’m also trying not to be close to the balloons. If I really have to stay, I keep as much distance as I can and just try to get through it without looking at them.\n\nDoctor: When you're in a situation where you see balloons and start feeling that chest tightness, do you also feel like it's hard to escape or get out of that situation, which makes the anxiety even worse?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s the “can’t escape” part exactly… I mean, at school I can usually move somewhere else, or I tell myself I’m just going to grab something from the supply closet. Although then I start thinking about whether I’m being obvious, because the kids notice everything—one time they all got very focused on a glitter jar I dropped, and then the whole morning was just chaos.\n\nBut with balloons, I guess I don’t really think in words like, “I’m trapped.” It’s more like I don’t want to be near it, and I’m very aware of where it is. If I can step away, I do. If I can’t, I just sort of try to keep doing what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried in situations that aren’t related to balloons, like during regular school activities or at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in the same way, I guess. I mean, school can be stressful just because there are a million little things happening at once—someone needs help with their shoes, someone spilled paint, someone is crying because their block tower fell over. So I do get frazzled, but that feels kind of normal for the job.\n\nAt home, I’m usually just tired. I’ll worry about whether I remembered to send a note to a parent or wash the snack containers, but… I don’t think it’s the same kind of anxious feeling. It’s hard to separate sometimes, though, because preschool days are just noisy and unpredictable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense wave of fear or discomfort that peaked within minutes when you were near balloons, where you felt like you were losing control or something terrible was going to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s like I’m losing control exactly… but it is really sudden. Like, the second I spot balloons at school, even if they’re all the way across the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ll be in the middle of thinking about attendance or snack time or whether someone left the paint cups out again, and then I see a balloon and it’s like my brain just stops for a second. I have to sort of collect myself before I can remember what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were near balloons and not only felt chest tightness and freezing up, but also noticed other strong physical or mental reactions—like feeling dizzy, short of breath, like your heart was pounding, or like you were detached from yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it dizziness or anything like being outside myself. What I notice most is that the second I see balloons—like even if they’re just across the school hallway—my chest tightens and I freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts lined back up.\n\nAnd then I’m standing there trying to remember what I was doing, which is ridiculous because it might just be, you know, taking the kids to wash hands or checking who has their coat on. But for that second, my brain kind of blanks before I can think clearly again.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel an overwhelming sense that something terrible is about to happen or that you might lose control, even just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think, like, “something terrible is going to happen” in words. It’s more that my body reacts before I’ve even caught up. The second I spot balloons at school—even if they’re way down the hallway—my chest tightens and I just freeze for a moment.\n\nThen I’m sort of standing there trying to remember what I was doing, like lining the kids up or checking backpacks, and it takes me a second to think clearly again. Which is hard, because in preschool there’s always something happening, someone needing help with a zipper or asking where the purple marker went… but yeah, the balloon thing just interrupts everything right away.\n\nDoctor: When you see balloons, do you ever feel like everything around you isn’t real, or like something really bad is about to happen to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like things look unreal or like I’m outside of myself. I’m not sure I’d describe it as “something bad is about to happen to me,” either.\n\nIt’s more just—well, balloons are everywhere at school sometimes, especially birthdays, and then there are all the little decorations people bring in, and I’m trying not to make it obvious because the kids get so excited about them. But no, I haven’t really noticed that unreal feeling you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness and freezing up, you also experienced at least three other symptoms all at once—like sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling dizzy, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’ve noticed that many things all happening at once. Like, I know I tense up and kind of stop for a second, but sweating or trembling or choking— I don’t really know.\n\nIt’s funny, because at school there are so many distractions that sometimes I’m more focused on keeping the kids from grabbing things or popping decorations, and then I’m thinking about whether I remembered snack count or if someone’s parent sent in cupcakes again. So I might not be paying attention to every body symptom, if that makes sense.\n\nBut no, I can’t really say there’s been a clear episode where I had three or more of those things all at once.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where, in addition to chest tightness when seeing balloons, you also experienced at least three other symptoms at the same time, like sweating, shaking, shortness of breath, dizziness, or feeling like you were choking?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you asked that already in a slightly different way. I don’t really have a clear answer beyond… I haven’t noticed three or more things like that happening all at once.\n\nWhen I see balloons, I definitely get stuck for a second and I’m aware of my chest, but then my brain usually jumps to, like, “Okay, where are the kids, is anyone about to touch it, do I need to move them along,” and then someone’s asking for help with their shoes or there’s glitter all over the table. So I’m not sitting there taking inventory of every symptom.\n\nBut no, I can’t point to a specific episode where I was also sweating and shaking and dizzy or choking or all of that together. I just don’t know that I’ve had it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or having physical symptoms like chest tightness in situations that aren't related to balloons—like during regular school activities, at home, or in other environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve really noticed in a clear way. Regular classroom stuff can be stressful, obviously—like when everyone needs something at the same time, or someone spills paint right after we cleaned up, or there’s a fire drill and half the class is crying—but I wouldn’t say I get that same chest feeling just from normal school things.\n\nAt home, I don’t think I notice it either. I mean, I can get tired or overwhelmed like anyone, especially after a long day with little kids, but… no, I can’t really point to physical symptoms happening in other situations the way they do when balloons are around.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about seeing balloons even when you’re not currently around them—like thinking ahead to school events or planning your day around the possibility of balloons being there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do think ahead about it sometimes. Like if I know there’s going to be a birthday in class, lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circle part, which feels a little silly because it’s just part of the day, but… if there might be balloons, I’d rather not be the one right next to them.\n\nAnd with school festivals or those little tables they set up—sometimes they’ll have balloons tied to signs or chairs, and I’ll sort of drift to the other side or find something else to do. It’s funny, because there’s always so much going on at those things, like parents asking where the bathrooms are or kids spilling juice, so I can make it look like I’m just busy.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay there, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at the balloons too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment when you suddenly felt intense fear or discomfort along with four or more of the following things happening at the same time: sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, feeling your heart race, chest pain, nausea, dizziness, chills or hot flashes, numbness, feeling detached from reality, or feeling like you were losing control or dying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve had it in that really sudden, all-at-once way you’re describing. Since then I’ve been reacting this way. So… yes, around seven months."
